{"type":"tool_call_update","toolCallId":"call-26b68a78-5412-4c97-8e3d-68cfab67ab5f-10","status":"in_progress","content":[{"type":"content","content":{"type":"text","text":"**Sliver check after every polish pass.** Faces under 1mm² are a polish bug unless\njustified. The only ones the doctrine allows outright are the corner triangles where\nthree 1mm chamfers meet at a convex corner, about 0.87mm². Anything else is usually\nchamfers colliding near a mating mouth, or a chamfered concave edge. A part declares the\ncount it has earned on its card, so a new one is a regression and a known one is silent.\n\n## Kernel rules\n\nSpecific to OCCT, and measured on real parts rather than reasoned about.\n\n- **Two chamfered convex edges need more than `2 * chamfer_size` of face between them.**\n  Closer and OCCT raises `BRep_API: command not done`, which build123d surfaces as\n  \"Failed creating a chamfer, try a smaller length value(s)\". At 1mm the shelf fails\n  with 1.66mm between the edges and builds with 2.16mm; at 0.5mm it builds with 1.16mm.\n  The threshold tracks the chamfer exactly. This is the single most common way a part\n  stops building, so `chamfer` says this much at the point of failure rather than\n  leaving the kernel's advice to stand: taking \"try a smaller length\" at its word is how\n  a part ends up with a 0.4mm chamfer that lands and then prints as a defect.\n- **One chamfered edge needs more than `chamfer_size` of face**, which is the same rule\n  with one chamfer instead of two and is easy to miss because it looks like plenty of\n  room. It bites wherever a polished edge sits beside something that is never polished:\n  a concave junction, a structural chamfer's toe, a pocket wall. Measured on three\n  different parts, all at the same threshold: 1.08mm builds and 1.00mm fails at a 1mm\n  chamfer.\n- **A batch chamfer failure is not a bad edge.** Every edge in a failing set chamfers\n  fine on its own, so testing them one at a time reports that nothing is wrong. Bisect\n  the set pairwise instead. \"Try a smaller length\" would never have found the rule above.\n- **The second way a chamfer dies, where bisecting does not help**, is an edge whose end\n  lands on a vertex with four faces around it and only three edges between them. Two of\n  those faces touch at a point without sharing an edge, and OCCT has no cap for that\n  corner. The edge fails on its own, on a clean body, at every length down to 0.05mm, so\n  nothing about the failure looks like a clearance problem. The fix is to chamfer in two\n  passes so that the first one gives those two faces a real edge to meet along, then\n  reselect from the result. Found on the parts bin, where the front drop and the side\n  taper land at the same height by design.\n- **Prefer `new_edges` over geometric selectors.** Each chamfer changes topology, so a\n  selector resolved against pristine geometry drifts once an earlier chamfer runs.\n  `new_edges(before, combined=after)` returns exactly the edges an operation created. It\n  is the algebra-mode equivalent of a builder's `Select.LAST`, and algebra mode is what a\n  part file uses, so `part.edges() - last` is not available. It is what makes a part\n  survive flexing its counts.\n- **A Fusion workaround is sometimes a real constraint wearing a disguise.** The gusset\n  drop that existed only to escape `ASM_BL_NO_MATE` turned out to be the same \"a chamfer\n  needs room to land\" constraint at a different number. Deleting it would have been\n  wrong; restating it as a rule was right.\n\n## Cards\n\nA part file carries geometry, not rationale. Every part worth keeping has a card next to\nit, same basename. **Read the card before editing the part, and update it when done.**\n\n"}}],"rawOutput":{"type":"Bash","output":[],"output_for_prompt":"**Sliver check after every polish pass.** Faces under 1mm² are a polish bug unless\njustified. This is the single most common way a part\n  stops building, so `chamfer` says this much at the point of failure rather than\n  leaving the kernel's advice to stand: taking \"try a smaller length\" at its word is how\n  a part ends up with a 0.4mm chamfer that lands and then prints as a defect.\n- **One chamfered edge needs more than `chamfer_size` of face**, which is the same rule\n  with one chamfer instead of two and is easy to miss because it looks like plenty of\n  room. It bites wherever a polished edge sits beside something that is never polished:\n  a concave junction, a structural chamfer's toe, a pocket wall. Measured on three\n  different parts, all at the same threshold: 1.08mm builds and 1.00mm fails at a 1mm\n  chamfer.\n- **A batch chamfer failure is not a bad edge.** Every edge in a failing set chamfers\n  fine on its own, so testing them one at a time reports that nothing is wrong. Bisect\n  the set pairwise instead. \"Try a smaller length\" would never have found the rule above.\n- **The second way a chamfer dies, where bisecting does not help**, is an edge whose end\n  lands on a vertex with four faces around it and only three edges between them. Two of\n  those faces touch at a point without sharing an edge, and OCCT has no cap for that\n  corner. The edge fails on its own, on a clean body, at every length down to 0.05mm, so\n  nothing about the failure looks like a clearance problem. The fix is to chamfer in two\n  passes so that the first one gives those two faces a real edge to meet along, then\n  reselect from the result. Found on the parts bin, where the front drop and the side\n  taper land at the same height by design.\n- **Prefer `new_edges` over geometric selectors.** Each chamfer changes topology, so a\n  selector resolved against pristine geometry drifts once an earlier chamfer runs.\n  `new_edges(before, combined=after)` returns exactly the edges an operation created. It\n  is the algebra-mode equivalent of a builder's `Select.LAST`, and algebra mode is what a\n  part file uses, so `part.edges() - last` is not available. It is what makes a part\n  survive flexing its counts.\n- **A Fusion workaround is sometimes a real constraint wearing a disguise.** The gusset\n  drop that existed only to escape `ASM_BL_NO_MATE` turned out to be the same \"a chamfer\n  needs room to land\" constraint at a different number. Deleting it would have been\n  wrong; restating it as a rule was right.\n\n## Cards\n\nA part file carries geometry, not rationale. Every part worth keeping has a card next to\nit, same basename. **Read the card before editing the part, and update it when done.**\n\n","exit_code":0,"command":"nurb rules 2>/dev/null | sed -n '200,250p'","truncated":false,"signal":null,"timed_out":false,"description":"Read kernel rules section of doctrine","current_dir":"/private/var/folders/s4/by90nw7j7_l_nvvqs3hd9mjh0000gn/T/nurb-eval-trial-v4kkjzxl/project","output_file":"/var/folders/s4/by90nw7j7_l_nvvqs3hd9mjh0000gn/T/nurb-grok-home-8r7xe692/sessions/%2Fprivate%2Fvar%2Ffolders%2Fs4%2Fby90nw7j7_l_nvvqs3hd9mjh0000gn%2FT%2Fnurb-eval-trial-v4kkjzxl%2Fproject/01a03563-9c73-7900-839e-6efac141a5ad/terminal/call-26b68a78-5412-4c97-8e3d-68cfab67ab5f-10.log","total_bytes":3519},"locations":[]}

It is a real number\n    that builds a real part; what it is not is measured, and `nurb check` says so until\n    somebody picks up a caliper.\n    \"\"\"\n    start = pathlib.Path(start).resolve() if start else _caller_dir(2)\n    path = _find(start)\n    if path is None:\n        raise MeasurementError(\n            f\"no {FILENAME} above {start}, so there is nothing measured to ask for. \"\n            f\"Measure {name!r}, then record it:\\n\\n\"\n            f'  [{name}]\\n  value = 0\\n  unit = \"mm\"\\n  how = \"how you measured it\"\\n\\n'\n            f\"If there is nobody to ask right now, add `provisional = true` and put \"\n            f\"your best guess in. `nurb check` will keep reminding you.\"\n        )\n    book = _read(path)\n    if name not in book:\n        have = \", \".join(sorted(book)) or \"nothing yet\"\n        raise MeasurementError(\n            f\"nothing measured called {name!r} in {path.name}. have: {have}. \"\n            f\"Ask for the measurement rather than guessing one: a wrong number here \"\n            f\"builds, checks clean, and prints.\"\n        )\n    entry = book[name]\n    if \"value\" not in entry:\n        raise MeasurementError(f\"{path.name}: {name} has no value\")\n    if \"how\" not in entry:\n        raise MeasurementError(\n            f\"{path.name}: {name} does not say how it was measured. A value without \"\n            f\"provenance is a guess with a filename on it.\"\n","total_lines":126}},"locations":[]}

The convexity test is here because a polish pass cannot\n# be written without it: chamfering an inside corner is the one polish mistake that looks\n# fine in code. `measured` is here because the alternative to asking for a dimension is\n# inventing one, and an invented one builds, checks clean and prints.\n# `stand` is here because a diagonal print variant cannot be written without it: the\n# rotation is trivial, but the bed facet it cuts is a doctrine rule (2mm of flat, or the\n# first layer is one extrusion line wide and peels), and a part file left to tilt by\n# hand ships the knife edge.\n# `crown` is here because rounding a variable-height rim by hand is an eight-step sweep\n40→# construction (issue #55), and every direct fillet of such a rim dies in OCCT's corner\n# capping. It is the doctrine's one sanctioned round-edge treatment.\n# `counterbore` is here because the naive alternative is two cylinders that build,\n# check as an ordinary bridge, and print a screw seat on sagging air: the stepped\n# bridging stack is fixed print-farm practice, not a judgement, so it is generated.\n# `reject` is here because a part that knows a configuration cannot work (a hole\n# narrower than the tool it holds) has to be able to say so, and the alternative is a\n# bare ValueError that the viewer can only present as a crash. One edge that cannot land takes the whole call down, and\nthe natural response is to shrink the set by hand until it builds. Shrinking it is the one response that makes a part quietly\n    worse, because a 0.4mm chamfer lands and then reads as a defect on the print.\n\n    So the rule goes here rather than only in `nurb rules`. This is the most common way\n    a part stops building, and an error is the one place a reader is guaranteed to be\n    looking.\n    \"\"\"\n    lines = [\n        \"\",\n40→        f\"  A {word} fails for room, not for length, and shrinking it is usually the\",\n        f\"  wrong fix. That is how a part\nwith ninety exposed edges ends up with three chamfered ones, and it is why the same\ndoctrine produces far more chamfers in a GUI, where a human adds them one feature at a\ntime and works around each collision individually.\n\nThe doctrine describes this algorithm and used to leave every project to write it. It\nwas 35 lines in one `system.py`, which meant the next project would write it again or,\nfar more likely, skip it and hand-narrow the set, which is exactly what the rule exists\nto prevent.\n\"\"\"\n\nimport collections.abc\nimport functools\n\nfrom build123d import chamfer as _chamfer\n\n# What OCCT says when a chamfer will not land. The same strings `nurb verify`\n# matches on, because both are asking the same question: did the kernel refuse, or did\n# the part refuse in its own words.\nKERNEL = (\"Failed creating a chamfer\", \"BRep_API\")\n\n\ndef _advice(count, size, word):\n    \"\"\"What the doctrine knows about this failure, said at the moment it happens.\n\n    The kernel's own message is \"try a smaller length value(s)\", and the doctrine is\n    explicit that following it would never have found the real rule: a chamfer fails\n    for room, not for length. Shrinking it is the one response that makes a part quietly\n    worse, because a 0.4mm chamfer lands and then reads as a defect on the print.\n\n    So the rule goes here rather than only in `nurb rules`. This is the most common way\n    a part stops building, and an error is the one place a reader is guaranteed to be\n    looking.\n    \"\"\"\n    lines = [\n        \"\",\n        f\"  A {word} fails for room, not for length, and shrinking it is usually the\",\n        f\"  wrong fix. Two {word}ed convex edges need more than {2 * size:g}mm of face between\",\n        f\"  them, and a single one needs more than {size:g}mm.\",\n    ]\n    if count > 1:\n        lines += [\n            \"\",\n            f\"  This call passed {count} edges as one batch, and a batch is all or nothing:\",\n            \"  one edge that cannot land takes the whole call down. Every edge in it very\",\n            \"  likely chamfers fine on its own, so testing them one at a time will report\",\n            \"  that nothing is wrong.\",\n            \"\",\n            # `!r` not `:g`: a size is a measurement, and this codebase reads a bare `1`\n            # as a count. Suggested code has to echo back the float the caller passed.\n            f\"  polish(shape, edges, {size!r}) bisects the set for you and keeps the halves that\",\n            \"  build, which is what the doctrine prescribes instead of narrowing by hand.\",\n        ]\n    else:\n        lines += [\n            \"\",\n            \"  A single edge that fails at every length is the other case: its end lands on\",\n            \"  a vertex with four faces and only three edges between them, and OCCT has no\",\n            \"  cap for that corner. Chamfer in two passes and reselect from the result.\",\n        ]\n    lines += [\"\", \"  `nurb rules` has both cases in full.\"]\n    return \"\\n\".join(lines)\n\n\ndef _count(objects):\n    try:\n        return len(list(objects))\n    except TypeError:  # a single Edge or Vertex, which is not iterable\n        return 1\n\n\ndef _guarded(fn, word, objects, size, *args, **kwargs):\n    # A generator would be exhausted by the failing call, so the count in the message\n    # would read zero and the advice would describe the wrong case. Same type and same opening line, so `nurb verify`'s kernel matching and any\n        # caller catching ValueError still work. `from None` because the original adds\n        # nothing a reader wants: it is the same sentence again under a C++ frame.\n        raise type(exc)(f\"{exc}\\n{_advice(_count(objects), size, word)}\") from None\n\n\n@functools.wraps(_chamfer)\ndef chamfer(objects, length, *args, **kwargs):\n    \"\"\"build123d's `chamfer`, with the doctrine's guidance on failure.\"\"\"\n    return _guarded(_chamfer, \"chamfer\", objects, length, *args, **kwargs)\n\n\ndef polish(shape, edges, size):\n    \"\"\"Chamfer as much of `edges` as will land, and stop there.\n\n    Try the set; where it fails, bisect and keep the halves that build.\n\n    **A batch is refused if it makes a face smaller than a corner triangle.** Three\n    chamfers meeting at a convex corner leave about `0.866 * size ** 2`, which the\n    doctrine allows outright; anything smaller is chamfers colliding, and that is what\n    \"keep chamfering until it stops working\" would otherwise walk straight into.\n    Counted as a delta, so geometry that was already small before the pass does not\n    veto every candidate.\n\n    Edges are taken longest first with ties broken on position, so the result does not\n    depend on the order they arrived in.\n\n    Every attempt chamfers the original solid with the whole accepted set at once, never\n    the result of the last attempt. An edge belongs to the shape it was selected from\n    and `chamfer` reads its target off that shape, so applying batches in sequence would\n    quietly keep re-chamfering the first body and hand it back.\n\n    This does not choose what to polish. Deciding which edges are exposed, which are\n    mating geometry and which are concave is the part's judgement and stays in the part.\n    \"\"\"\n    floor = 0.8 * size**2\n\n    def tiny(solid):\n        return len([f for f in solid.faces() if f.area < floor])\n\n    allowance = tiny(shape)\n    kept, best = [], shape\n\n    def lands(candidate):\n        try:\n            # The raw kernel call, not the guarded one: bisecting expects failures and\n            # discards them, so building the guidance text here would be advice nobody\n            # reads, once per attempt.\n            out = _chamfer(candidate, size)\n        except Exception:\n            return None\n        return out if tiny(out) <= allowance else None\n\n    def take(batch):\n        nonlocal kept, best\n        out = lands(kept + batch)\n        if out is not None:\n            kept, best = kept + batch, out\n            return\n        if len(batch) == 1:\n            return  # this one cannot land beside what is already there\n        half = len(batch) // 2\n        take(batch[:half])\n        take(batch[half:])\n\n    take(sorted(edges, key=lambda e: (-e.length, e.center().X, e.center().Y, e.center().Z)))\n    return best\n","total_lines":153}},"locations":[]}

Must stay in [0.1, 0.4] so the\n# pole is clear of the rest but still sitting in a close cradle.\nCLEARANCE = 0.2\nWALL = 3.5\nLENGTH = 24.0\nPOLISH = 1.0\n\n\n@part\ndef pole_rest(pole_diameter=20.0, draft=False):\n    \"\"\"Drying rest that cradles a freshly finished pole.\n\n    pole_diameter: width of the pole this rest holds\n    \"\"\"\n    if pole_diameter < 8.0:\n        reject(\n            f\"pole_diameter {pole_diameter} is too small for a printable cradle; \"\n            \"raise it above 8\",\n            param=\"pole_diameter\",\n        )\n\n    inner_r = pole_diameter / 2.0 + CLEARANCE\n    floor = AXIS_HEIGHT - inner_r\n    if floor < 2.4:\n        limit = 2.0 * (AXIS_HEIGHT - 2.4 - CLEARANCE)\n        reject(\n            f\"pole_diameter {pole_diameter} puts the cradle within 2.4mm of the bed; \"\n            f\"keep it at or below {limit:.1f}\",\n            param=\"pole_diameter\",\n        )\n\n    width = 2.0 * (inner_r + WALL)\n    height = AXIS_HEIGHT\n\n    body = Pos(0, 0, height / 2.0) * Box(width, LENGTH, height)\n    cutter = Pos(0, 0, AXIS_HEIGHT) * (Rot(90, 0, 0) * Cylinder(inner_r, LENGTH + 2.0))\n    body = body - cutter\n\n    if draft:\n        return body\n\n    bed = body.bounding_box().min.Z\n    skip = concave_edges(body)\n    keep = [\n        e\n        for e in body.edges()\n        if e.bounding_box().max.Z > bed + 0.05 and e not in skip\n    ]\n    return polish(body, keep, POLISH)\n"},"content":[],"locations":[]}

{"type":"tool_call","toolCallId":"call-90f36f3c-9c4d-40dd-89c9-bcaebe7cd489-24","title":"write","kind":"write","status":"pending","toolName":"write","rawInput":{"file_path":"/private/var/folders/s4/by90nw7j7_l_nvvqs3hd9mjh0000gn/T/nurb-eval-trial-v4kkjzxl/project/parts/pole_rest.md","content":"# pole_rest\n\nA bench rest that holds a freshly finished pole while it dries. Several stand in a row; the pole lies across them along Y.\n\n## What it is\n\nA short block with a half-pipe cradle. The pole sits in the trough, axis along Y at 18mm above the bed, centred on the part in X. Printed and used sitting on the bed.\n\n## Design notes\n\n- `pole_diameter` is the measured width of the pole (20.0mm in measurements.toml). The cradle radius is that plus 0.2mm so the finish does not touch the plastic.\n- Axis height is fixed at 18.0mm for every diameter, because a row of rests has to line up.\n- The block top is at the axis so the cut is a true half-pipe: 180° of close cradle, open from above so the pole drops in.\n- Walls are 3.5mm so a 1mm polish still leaves backing behind the contact.\n\n## Don't\n\n- Do not close over the equator. A wrap past 180° pinches the opening and the pole cannot drop in.\n- Do not use a V-block or knife-edge saddle. The finish is soft; it needs a cradle near the pole's own radius, not two lines of contact.\n- Do not polish the concave trough ends. Chamfering those circular edges pulls the cradle off the pole at the ends.\n\n## Changelog\n\n- Half-pipe rest derived from pole_diameter, axis locked at 18mm.\n"},"content":[],"locations":[]}
{"type":"tool_call_update","toolCallId":"call-90f36f3c-9c4d-40dd-89c9-bcaebe7cd489-24","status":null,"content":[{"type":"diff","path":"/private/var/folders/s4/by90nw7j7_l_nvvqs3hd9mjh0000gn/T/nurb-eval-trial-v4kkjzxl/project/parts/pole_rest.md","oldText":"","newText":"# pole_rest\n\nA bench rest that holds a freshly finished pole while it dries. Several stand in a row; the pole lies across them along Y.\n\n## What it is\n\nA short block with a half-pipe cradle. The pole sits in the trough, axis along Y at 18mm above the bed, centred on the part in X. Printed and used sitting on the bed.\n\n## Design notes\n\n- `pole_diameter` is the measured width of the pole (20.0mm in measurements.toml). The cradle radius is that plus 0.2mm so the finish does not touch the plastic.\n- Axis height is fixed at 18.0mm for every diameter, because a row of rests has to line up.\n- The block top is at the axis so the cut is a true half-pipe: 180° of close cradle, open from above so the pole drops in.\n- Walls are 3.5mm so a 1mm polish still leaves backing behind the contact.\n\n## Don't\n\n- Do not close over the equator. A wrap past 180° pinches the opening and the pole cannot drop in.\n- Do not use a V-block or knife-edge saddle. The finish is soft; it needs a cradle near the pole's own radius, not two lines of contact.\n- Do not polish the concave trough ends. Chamfering those circular edges pulls the cradle off the pole at the ends.\n\n## Changelog\n\n- Half-pipe rest derived from pole_diameter, axis locked at 18mm.\n"}],"rawOutput":null,"locations":[{"path":"/private/var/folders/s4/by90nw7j7_l_nvvqs3hd9mjh0000gn/T/nurb-eval-trial-v4kkjzxl/project/parts/pole_rest.md"}]}

Several stand in a row; the pole lies across them along Y.\n\n10→## What it is\n\nA short block with a half-pipe cradle. The pole sits in the trough, axis along Y at 18mm above the bed, centred on the part in X. Printed and used sitting on the bed.\n\n## Design notes\n\n- `pole_diameter` is the measured width of the pole (20.0mm in measurements.toml). The cradle radius is that plus 0.2mm so the finish does not touch the plastic.\n- Axis height is fixed at 18.0mm for every diameter, because a row of rests has to line up.\n- The block top is at the axis so the cut is a true half-pipe: 180° of close cradle, open from above so the pole drops in.\n- Walls are 3.5mm so a 1mm polish still leaves backing behind the contact.\n20→\n## Don't\n\n- Do not close over the equator. A wrap past 180° pinches the opening and the pole cannot drop in.\n- Do not use a V-block or knife-edge saddle. The finish is soft; it needs a cradle near the pole's own radius, not two lines of contact.\n- Do not polish the concave trough ends. The cradle radius is that plus 0.2mm so the finish does not touch the plastic.\n- Axis height is fixed at 18.0mm for every diameter, because a row of rests has to line up.\n- The block top is at the axis so the cut is a true half-pipe: 180° of close cradle, open from above so the pole drops in.\n- Walls are 3.5mm so a 1mm polish still leaves backing behind the contact.\n\n## Don't\n\n- Do not close over the equator.
